∎

Theorem 5.15 (Complex CBPV is Conservative over CBPV).

If
M,M^{\prime}
are terms in CBPV and
M\sqsubseteq M^{\prime}
is provable in CBPV* then
M\sqsubseteq M^{\prime}
is provable in CBPV.

Proof.

Because de-complexification preserves dynamism,
{M}^{{\dagger}}\sqsubseteq{M^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}
in simple CBPV. Then it follows because
de-complexification is equivalent to identity (in CBPV):

M\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}{M}^{{\dagger}}\sqsubseteq{M^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}M^{\prime}

∎

6.
Operational Model of GTT

In this section, we establish a model of our CBPV inequational theory
using a notion of observational approximation based on the CBPV
operational semantics.
By composition with the axiomatic graduality theorem, this establishes
the
operational graduality
theorem, i.e., a theorem analogous
to the
dynamic gradual guarantee

(
Siek
et al. 2015a
)
.

6.1.
Call-by-push-value operational semantics

We use a small-step operational semantics for CBPV
in figure

15
.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}S[\mho]&\mathrel{\mapsto^{0}}&\mho\\
S[\texttt{case}\,\,\texttt{inl}\,\,V\{x_{1}.M_{1}\,\,|\,\,x_{2}.M_{2}\}]&\mathrel{\mapsto^{0}}&S[M_{1}[V/x_{1}]]\\
S[\texttt{case}\,\,\texttt{inr}\,\,V\{x_{1}.M_{1}\,\,|\,\,x_{2}.M_{2}\}]&\mathrel{\mapsto^{0}}&S[M_{2}[V/x_{2}]]\\
S[\texttt{split}\,\,(V_{1},V_{2})\,\texttt{to}\,\,(x_{1},x_{2}).M]&\mathrel{\mapsto^{0}}&S[M[V_{1}/x_{1},V_{2}/x_{2}]]\\
S[\texttt{unroll}\,\,\texttt{roll}_{A}\,\,V\,\texttt{to}\,\,\texttt{roll}\,\,x.M]&\mathrel{\mapsto^{1}}&S[M[V/x]]\\
S[\texttt{force}\,\,\texttt{thunk}\,\,M]&\mathrel{\mapsto^{0}}&S[M]\\
S[\texttt{let}\,\,x=V;M]&\mathrel{\mapsto^{0}}&S[M[V/x]]\\
S[\texttt{bind}\,\,x\leftarrow\texttt{ret}\,\,V;M]&\mathrel{\mapsto^{0}}&S[M[V/x]]\\
S[(\lambda x:A.M)\,V]&\mathrel{\mapsto^{0}}&S[M[V/x]]\\
S[\pi\{\pi\mapsto{M}\,\,|\,\,\pi^{\prime}\mapsto{M^{\prime}}\}]&\mathrel{\mapsto^{0}}&S[M]\\
S[\pi^{\prime}\{\pi\mapsto{M}\,\,|\,\,\pi^{\prime}\mapsto{M^{\prime}}\}]&\mathrel{\mapsto^{0}}&S[M^{\prime}]\\
S[\texttt{unroll}\,\,\texttt{roll}_{\underline{B}}\,\,M]&\mathrel{\mapsto^{1}}&S[M]\\
\end{array}

\frac{}{M\mathrel{\Mapsto^{0}}M}\par\vskip 12.02495pt\par\frac{M_{1}\mathrel{\mapsto^{i}}M_{2}M_{2}\mathrel{\Mapsto^{j}}M_{3}}{M_{1}\mathrel{\Mapsto^{i+j}}M_{3}}

Figure 15.
CBPV Operational Semantics

This is morally the same as in
Levy 2003
, but we present
stacks in a manner similar to Hieb-Felleisen style evaluation
contexts(rather than as an explicit stack machine with stack frames).
We also make the step relation count unrollings of a recursive or
corecursive type, for the step-indexed logical relation later.
The operational semantics is only defined for terms of type

\cdot\vdash M:\underline{F}(1+1)
, which we take as the type of whole
programs.

We can then observe the following standard operational properties. (We
write
M\mapsto N
with no index when the index is irrelevant.)

Lemma 6.1 (Reduction is Deterministic).

If
M\mapsto M_{1}
and
M\mapsto M_{2}
, then
M_{1}=M_{2}
.

Lemma 6.2 (Subject Reduction).

If
\cdot\vdash M:\underline{F}A
and
M\mapsto M^{\prime}
then

\cdot\vdash M^{\prime}:\underline{F}A
.

Lemma 6.3 (Progress).

If
\cdot\vdash M:\underline{F}A
then one of the following holds:

M=\mho M=\texttt{ret}\,\,V\text{with}V:A\exists M^{\prime}.~M\mapsto M^{\prime}

The standard progress-and-preservation properties allow us to define
the “final result” of a computation as follows:

Corollary 6.4 (Possible Results of Computation).

For any
\cdot\vdash M:\underline{F}2
,
one of the following is true:

M\Uparrow M\Downarrow\mho M\Downarrow\texttt{ret}\,\,\texttt{true}M\Downarrow\texttt{ret}\,\,\texttt{false}

Proof.

We define
M\Uparrow
to hold when if
M\mathrel{\Mapsto^{i}}N
then
there exists
N^{\prime}
with
N\mapsto N^{\prime}
. For the terminating results, we
define
M\Downarrow R
to hold if there exists some
i
with
M\mathrel{\Mapsto^{i}}R
. Then we prove the result by coinduction on
execution traces. If
M\in\{\mho,\texttt{ret}\,\,\texttt{true},\texttt{ret}\,\,\texttt{false}\}
then we
are done, otherwise by progress,
M\mapsto M^{\prime}
, so we need only
observe that each of the cases above is preserved by
\mapsto
.∎

Definition 6.5 (Results).

The possible results of a computation are
\Omega,\mho,\texttt{ret}\,\,\texttt{true}
and
ret

false
. We denote a result by
R
, and define a
function
result
which takes a program
\cdot\vdash M:\underline{F}2
,
and returns its end-behavior, i.e.,
\text{result}(M)=\Omega
if
M\Uparrow
and otherwise
M\Downarrow\text{result}(M)
.

6.2.
Observational Equivalence and Approximation

Next, we define observational equivalence and approximation in CBPV.
The (standard) definition of observational equivalence is that we
consider two terms (or values) to be equivalent when replacing one
with the other in any program text produces the same overall resulting
computation.
Define a context
C
to be a term/value/stack with a single
[\cdot]
as
some subterm/value/stack, and define a typing
C:(\Gamma\vdash\underline{B})\Rightarrow(\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\underline{B}^{\prime})
to hold when for any
\Gamma\vdash M:\underline{B}
,
\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash C[M]:\underline{B}^{\prime}
(and similarly for
values/stacks). Using contexts, we can lift any relation on

results
to relations on open terms, values and stacks.

Definition 6.6 (Contextual Lifting).

Given any relation
{\sim}\subseteq\text{Result}^{2}
, we can define
its
observational lift

\mathrel{{\sim}^{\text{ctx}}}
to be the typed relation
defined by

\Gamma\,\,|\,\,\Delta\vDash E\mathrel{{\sim}^{\text{ctx}}}E^{\prime}\in T=\forall C:(\Gamma\,\,|\,\,\Delta\vdash T)\Rightarrow(\cdot\vdash\underline{F}2).~\text{result}(C[E])\sim\text{result}(C[E^{\prime}])

\begin{array}[]{rcl}C_{V}&::=[\cdot]&\texttt{roll}_{\mu X.A}\,\,C_{V}\mid\texttt{inl}\,\,{C_{V}}\mid\texttt{inr}\,\,{C_{V}}\mid(C_{V},V)\mid(V,C_{V})\mid\texttt{thunk}\,\,{C_{M}}\\
\\
C_{M}&::=&[\cdot]\mid\texttt{let}\,\,x=C_{V};M\mid\texttt{let}\,\,x=V;C_{M}\mid\texttt{unroll}\,\,C_{V}\,\texttt{to}\,\,\texttt{roll}\,\,x.M\mid\texttt{unroll}\,\,V\,\texttt{to}\,\,\texttt{roll}\,\,x.C_{M}\\
&&\mid\texttt{roll}_{\nu\underline{Y}.\underline{B}}\,\,C_{M}\mid\texttt{unroll}\,\,C_{M}\mid\texttt{abort}\,\,{C_{V}}\mid\texttt{case}\,\,C_{V}\{x_{1}.M_{1}\,\,|\,\,x_{2}.M_{2}\}\\
&&\mid\texttt{case}\,\,V\{x_{1}.C_{M}\,\,|\,\,x_{2}.M_{2}\}\mid\texttt{case}\,\,V\{x_{1}.M_{1}\,\,|\,\,x_{2}.C_{M}\}\mid\texttt{split}\,\,C_{V}\,\texttt{to}\,\,().M\\
&&\mid\texttt{split}\,\,V\,\texttt{to}\,\,().C_{M}\mid\texttt{split}\,\,C_{V}\,\texttt{to}\,\,(x,y).M\mid\texttt{split}\,\,V\,\texttt{to}\,\,(x,y).C_{M}\mid\texttt{force}\,\,{C_{V}}\\
&&\mid\texttt{ret}\,\,{C_{V}}\mid\texttt{bind}\,\,x\leftarrow C_{M};{N}\mid\texttt{bind}\,\,x\leftarrow M;{C_{M}}\mid\lambda x:A.C_{M}\mid C_{M}\,V\mid M\,C_{V}\\
&&\mid\{\pi\mapsto{C_{M}}\,\,|\,\,\pi^{\prime}\mapsto{M_{2}}\}\mid\{\pi\mapsto{M_{1}}\,\,|\,\,\pi^{\prime}\mapsto{C_{M}}\}\mid\pi C_{M}\mid\pi^{\prime}C_{M}\\
C_{S}&=&\pi C_{S}\mid\pi^{\prime}C_{S}\mid S\,C_{V}\mid C_{S}\,V\mid\texttt{bind}\,\,x\leftarrow C_{S};M\mid\texttt{bind}\,\,x\leftarrow S;C_{M}\end{array}

Figure 16.
CBPV Contexts

The contextual lifting
\mathrel{{\sim}^{\text{ctx}}}
inherits much structure of the
original relation
\sim
as the following lemma shows.
This justifies calling
\mathrel{{\sim}^{\text{ctx}}}
a contextual preorder when
\sim

is a preorder (reflexive and transitive) and similarly a contextual
equivalence when
\sim
is an equivalence (preorder and symmetric).

Definition 6.7 (Contextual Preorder, Equivalence).

If
\sim
is reflexive, symmetric or transitive, then for each
typing,
\mathrel{{\sim}^{\text{ctx}}}
is reflexive, symmetric or transitive as well,
respectively.

In the remainder of the paper we work only with relations that are at
least preorders so we write
\trianglelefteq
rather than
\sim
.

The most famous use of lifting is for observational equivalence,
which is the lifting of equality of results (
\mathrel{{=}^{\text{ctx}}}
), and we will show that

\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}
proofs in GTT imply observational equivalences.
However, as shown in
New and Ahmed 2018
, the graduality property is
defined in terms of an observational
approximation
relation

\sqsubseteq
that places
\mho
as the least element, and every other
element as a maximal element.
Note that this is
not
the standard notion of observational
approximation, which we write
\preceq
, which makes
\Omega
a least
element and every other element a maximal element.
To distinguish these, we call
\sqsubseteq

error
approximation and

\preceq

divergence
approximation.
We present these graphically (with two more) in Figure

17
.

Diverge Approx.
\preceq

ret

false
ret

true
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mho}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}

Error Approx.
\sqsubseteq
ret

false
ret

true
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mho}

Error Approx. up to left-divergence

\preceq\sqsubseteq

ret

false
ret

true
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mho,\Omega}

Error Approx. up to right-divergence

\sqsubseteq\succeq
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}
ret

false
ret

true
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mho}

Error Approx. up to right-divergence Op

\preceq\sqsupseteq
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mho}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega}

Figure 17.
Result Orderings

The goal of this section is to prove that a symmetric equality
E\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}E^{\prime}
in CBPV (i.e.
E\sqsubseteq E^{\prime}
and
E^{\prime}\sqsubseteq E
) implies contextual
equivalence
E\mathrel{{=}^{\text{ctx}}}E^{\prime}
and that inequality in CBPV
E\sqsubseteq E^{\prime}

implies error approximation
E\mathrel{{\sqsubseteq}^{\text{ctx}}}E^{\prime}
, proving graduality of the operational model:

\frac{\Gamma\,\,|\,\,\Delta\vdash E\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}E^{\prime}:T}{\Gamma\,\,|\,\,\Delta\vDash E\mathrel{{=}^{\text{ctx}}}E^{\prime}\in T}\frac{\Gamma\,\,|\,\,\Delta\vdash E\sqsubseteq E^{\prime}:T}{\Gamma\,\,|\,\,\Delta\vDash E\mathrel{{\sqsubseteq}^{\text{ctx}}}E^{\prime}\in T}

Because we have non-well-founded
\mu/\nu
types, we use a

step-indexed logical relation
to prove properties about the
contextual lifting of certain preorders
\trianglelefteq
on results.
In step-indexing, the
infinitary
relation given by

\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{ctx}}}
is related to the set of all of its
finitary
approximations

\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}
, which “time out” after observing

i
steps of evaluation and declare that the
terms
are
related.

This means that the original relation is only recoverable from the
finite approximations if
\Omega
is always related to another
element: if the relation is a preorder, we require that
\Omega
is
a
least
element.

We call such a preorder a
divergence preorder
.

Definition 6.8 (Divergence Preorder).

A preorder on results
\trianglelefteq
is a divergence preorder if

\Omega\trianglelefteq R
for all results
R
.

But this presents a problem, because
neither
of our intended
relations (
=
and
\sqsubseteq
) is a divergence preorder; rather both have

\Omega
as a
maximal
element.

However, there is a standard “trick” for subverting this obstacle in
the case of contextual equivalence
(
Ahmed 2006
)
: we notice
that we can define equivalence as the symmetrization of divergence
approximation, i.e.,
M\mathrel{{=}^{\text{ctx}}}N
if and only if
M\mathrel{{\preceq}^{\text{ctx}}}N
and
N\mathrel{{\preceq}^{\text{ctx}}}M
, and since
\preceq
has
\Omega
as
a least element, we can use a step-indexed relation to prove it.
As shown in
New and Ahmed 2018
, a similar trick works for error
approximation, but since
\sqsubseteq
is
not
an equivalence
relation, we decompose it rather into two
different
orderings:
error approximation up to divergence on the left
\preceq\sqsubseteq
and
error approximation up to divergence on the right
\sqsubseteq\succeq
,
also shown in figure
17
.
Note that
\preceq\sqsubseteq
is a preorder, but not a poset because

\mho,\Omega
are order-equivalent but not equal.
Then clearly
\preceq\sqsubseteq
is a divergence preorder and the

opposite
of
\sqsubseteq\succeq
, written
\preceq\sqsupseteq

is a divergence preorder.

Then we can completely reduce the problem of proving
\mathrel{{=}^{\text{ctx}}}
and

\mathrel{{\sqsubseteq}^{\text{ctx}}}
results to proving results about divergence preorders
by the following observations.

Lemma 6.9 (Decomposing Result Preorders).

Let
R,S
be results.

(1)

R=S
if and only if
R\sqsubseteq S
and
S\sqsubseteq R
.

(2)

R=S
if and only if
R\preceq S
and
S\preceq R
.

(3)

R\preceq\sqsubseteq S
iff
R\sqsubseteq S
or
R\preceq S
.

(4)

R\sqsubseteq\succeq S
iff
R\sqsubseteq S
or
R\succeq S
.

In the following, we write
\sim^{\circ}
for the opposite of a relation
(
x\sim^{\circ}y
iff
y\sim x
),
\Rightarrow
for
containment/implication (
\sim\Rightarrow\sim^{\prime}
iff
x\sim y
implies

x\sim^{\prime}y
),
\Leftrightarrow
for bicontainment/equality,
\vee
for
union (
x(\sim\vee\sim^{\prime})y
iff
x\sim y
or
x\sim^{\prime}y
), and

\wedge
for intersection (
x(\sim\wedge\sim^{\prime})y
iff
x\sim y
and
x\sim^{\prime}y
).

Lemma 6.10 (Contextual Lift commutes with Conjunction).

\mathrel{{(\mathrel{\sim_{1}}\wedge\mathrel{\sim_{2}})}^{\text{ctx}}}\Leftrightarrow\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sim_{1}}}^{\text{ctx}}}\wedge\mathrel{{\mathrel{\sim_{2}}}^{\text{ctx}}}

Lemma 6.11 (Contextual Lift commutes with Dualization).

\mathrel{{\sim^{\circ}}^{\text{ctx}}}\Leftrightarrow\mathrel{{\sim}^{\text{ctx}}}^{\circ}

Lemma 6.12 (Contextual Decomposition Lemma).

Let
\sim
be a reflexive relation
(=\Rightarrow\sim)
, and
\leqslant

be a reflexive, antisymmetric relation (
{=}\Rightarrow{\leqslant}
and

(\leqslant\wedge{\leqslant^{\circ}})\Leftrightarrow{=}
). Then

\mathrel{{\sim}^{\text{ctx}}}\Leftrightarrow\mathrel{{(\sim\vee\leqslant)}^{\text{ctx}}}\wedge(\mathrel{{(\sim^{\circ}\vee\leqslant)}^{\text{ctx}}})^{\circ}

Proof.

Note that despite the notation,
\leqslant
need not be assumed to be
transitive.
Reflexive relations form a lattice with
\wedge
and
\vee
with
=
as

\bot
and the total relation as
\top
(e.g.
(=\vee\sim)\Leftrightarrow\sim
because
\sim
is reflexive, and
(=\wedge\sim)\Leftrightarrow=
). So we have

\sim\Leftrightarrow(\sim\vee\leqslant)\wedge(\sim\vee\leqslant^{\circ})

because FOILing the right-hand side gives

(\sim\wedge\sim)\vee(\leqslant\wedge\sim)\vee(\sim\wedge\leqslant^{\circ})\vee(\leqslant\wedge\leqslant^{\circ})

By antisymmetry,
(\leqslant\wedge\leqslant^{\circ})
is
=
, which is the
unit of
\vee
, so it cancels. By idempotence,
(\sim\wedge\sim)
is
\sim
.
Then by absorption, the whole thing is
\sim
.

Opposite is
not
de Morgan:
(P\vee Q)^{\circ}=P^{\circ}\vee Q^{\circ}
, and similarly for
\wedge
. But it is involutive:

(P^{\circ})^{\circ}\Leftrightarrow P
.

So using Lemmas
6.10
,
6.11
we can calculate as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathrel{{\sim}^{\text{ctx}}}&\Leftrightarrow&\mathrel{{((\sim\vee\leqslant)\wedge(\sim\vee\leqslant^{\circ}))}^{\text{ctx}}}\\
&\Leftrightarrow&\mathrel{{(\sim\vee\leqslant)}^{\text{ctx}}}\wedge\mathrel{{(\sim\vee\leqslant^{\circ})}^{\text{ctx}}}\\
&\Leftrightarrow&\mathrel{{(\sim\vee\leqslant)}^{\text{ctx}}}\wedge\mathrel{{((\sim\vee\leqslant^{\circ})^{\circ})^{\circ}}^{\text{ctx}}}\\
&\Leftrightarrow&\mathrel{{(\sim\vee\leqslant)}^{\text{ctx}}}\wedge\mathrel{{((\sim^{\circ}\vee(\leqslant^{\circ})^{\circ})^{\circ})}^{\text{ctx}}}\\
&\Leftrightarrow&\mathrel{{(\sim\vee\leqslant)}^{\text{ctx}}}\wedge\mathrel{{(\sim^{\circ}\vee\leqslant)^{\circ}}^{\text{ctx}}}\\
&\Leftrightarrow&\mathrel{{(\sim\vee\leqslant)}^{\text{ctx}}}\wedge\mathrel{{(\sim^{\circ}\vee\leqslant)}^{\text{ctx}}}^{\circ}\end{array}

∎

As a corollary, the decomposition of contextual equivalence into diverge
approximation in
Ahmed 2006
and the decomposition of dynamism in

New and Ahmed 2018
are really the same trick:

Corollary 6.13 (Contextual Decomposition).

(1)

\mathrel{{=}^{\text{ctx}}}\mathbin{\Leftrightarrow}\mathrel{{\preceq}^{\text{ctx}}}\wedge(\mathrel{{(\preceq)}^{\text{ctx}}})^{\circ}

(2)

\mathrel{{=}^{\text{ctx}}}\mathbin{\Leftrightarrow}\mathrel{{\sqsubseteq}^{\text{ctx}}}\wedge(\mathrel{{(\sqsubseteq)}^{\text{ctx}}})^{\circ}

(3)

\mathrel{{\sqsubseteq}^{\text{ctx}}}\mathbin{\Leftrightarrow}\mathrel{{\preceq\sqsubseteq}^{\text{ctx}}}\wedge(\mathrel{{(\preceq\sqsupseteq)}^{\text{ctx}}})^{\circ}

Proof.

For part 1 (though we will not use this below), applying
Lemma
6.12
with
\sim
taken to be
=

(which is reflexive) and
\leqslant
taken to be
\preceq
(which is
reflexive and antisymmetric) gives that contextual equivalence is
symmetric contextual divergence approximation:

\mathrel{{=}^{\text{ctx}}}\Leftrightarrow\mathrel{{(=\vee\preceq)}^{\text{ctx}}}\wedge(\mathrel{{(=^{\circ}\vee\preceq)}^{\text{ctx}}})^{\circ}\Leftrightarrow\mathrel{{\preceq}^{\text{ctx}}}\wedge(\mathrel{{(\preceq)}^{\text{ctx}}})^{\circ}

For part (2), the same argument with
\sim
taken to be
=
and

\leqslant
taken to be
\sqsubseteq
(which is also antisymmetric) gives that
contextual equivalence is symmetric contextual dynamism:

\mathrel{{=}^{\text{ctx}}}\Leftrightarrow\mathrel{{\sqsubseteq}^{\text{ctx}}}\wedge(\mathrel{{(\sqsubseteq)}^{\text{ctx}}})^{\circ}

For part (3), applying Lemma
6.12
with
\sim

taken to be
\sqsubseteq
and
\leqslant
taken to be
\preceq
gives that
dynamism decomposes as

\mathrel{{\sqsubseteq}^{\text{ctx}}}\Leftrightarrow\mathrel{{(\sqsubseteq\vee\preceq)}^{\text{ctx}}}\wedge(\mathrel{{(\sqsubseteq^{\circ}\vee\preceq)}^{\text{ctx}}})^{\circ}\Leftrightarrow\mathrel{{\preceq\sqsubseteq}^{\text{ctx}}}\wedge(\mathrel{{(\preceq\sqsupseteq)}^{\text{ctx}}})^{\circ}

Since both
{\preceq\sqsubseteq}
and
\preceq\sqsupseteq
are of the
form
-\vee\preceq
, both are divergence preorders. Thus, it suffices
to develop logical relations for divergence preorders below.
∎

6.3.
CBPV Step Indexed Logical Relation

Next, we turn to the problem of proving results about
E\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{ctx}}}E^{\prime}
where
\trianglelefteq
is a divergence preorder.
Dealing directly with a contextual preorder is practically impossible,
so instead we develop an alternative formulation as a logical relation
that is much easier to use.
Fortunately, we can apply standard logical relations techniques to
provide an alternate definition
inductively
on types.
However, since we have non-well-founded type definitions using

\mu
and
\nu
, our logical relation will also be defined inductively on a

step index
that times out when we’ve exhausted our step budget.
To bridge the gap between the indexed logical relation and the
divergence preorder we care about, we define the “finitization” of a
divergence preorder to be a relation between
programs
and

results
: the idea is that a program approximates a result
R

at index
i
if it reduces to
R
in less than
i
steps or it reduces
at least
i
times.

Definition 6.14 (Finitized Preorder).

Given a divergence preorder
\trianglelefteq
, we define the

finitization
of
\trianglelefteq
to be, for each natural number

i
, a relation between programs and results

{\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}}\subseteq\{M\,\,|\,\,\cdot\vdash M:\underline{F}2\}\times\text{Results}

defined by

M\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}R=(\exists M^{\prime}.~M\mathrel{\Mapsto^{i}}M^{\prime})\vee(\exists(j<i).\exists R_{M}.~M\mathrel{\Mapsto^{j}}R_{M}\wedge R_{M}\trianglelefteq R)

Note that in this definition, unlike in the definition of divergence,
we only count non-well-founded steps.
This makes it slightly harder to establish the intended equivalence
M\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{\omega}}R
if and only if
\text{result}(M)\trianglelefteq R
, but
makes the logical relation theorem stronger: it proves that diverging
terms must use recursive types of some sort and so any term that does
not use them terminates.
This issue would be alleviated if we had proved type safety by a
logical relation rather than by progress and preservation.

However, the following properties of the indexed relation can easily
be established.
First, a kind of “transitivity” of the indexed relation with respect
to the original preorder, which is key to proving transitivity of the
logical relation.

Lemma 6.15 (Indexed Relation is a Module of the Preorder).

If
M\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}R
and
R\trianglelefteq R^{\prime}
then
M\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}R^{\prime}

Proof.

If
M\mathrel{\Mapsto^{i}}M^{\prime}
then there’s nothing to show, otherwise

M\mathrel{\Mapsto^{j<i}}\text{result}(M)
so it follows by transitivity of the
preorder:
\text{result}(M)\trianglelefteq R\trianglelefteq R^{\prime}
.∎

Then we establish a few basic properties of the finitized preorder.

Lemma 6.16 (Downward Closure of Finitized Preorder).

If
M\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}R
and
j\leq i
then
M\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{j}}R
.

Proof.

(1)

If
M\mathrel{\Mapsto^{i}}M_{i}
then
M\mathrel{\Mapsto^{j}}M_{j}
and otherwise

(2)

If
M\mathrel{\Mapsto^{j\leq ki}}\text{result}(M)
then
M\mathrel{\Mapsto^{j}}M_{j}

(3)

if
M\mathrel{\Mapsto^{k<j\leq i}}\text{result}(M)
then
\text{result}(M)\trianglelefteq R
.

∎

Lemma 6.17 (Triviality at
0
).

For any
\cdot\vdash M:\underline{F}2
,
M\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{0}}R

Proof.

Because
M\mathrel{\Mapsto^{0}}M
∎

Lemma 6.18 (Result (Anti-)reduction).

If
M\mathrel{\Mapsto^{i}}N
then
\text{result}(M)=\text{result}(N)
.

Lemma 6.19 (Anti-reduction).

If
M\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}R
and
N\mathrel{\Mapsto^{j}}M
, then
N\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{{i+j}}}R

Proof.

(1)

If
M\mathrel{\Mapsto^{i}}M^{\prime}
then
N\mathrel{\Mapsto^{i+j}}M^{\prime}

(2)

If
M\mathrel{\Mapsto^{k<i}}\text{result}(M)
then
N\mathrel{\Mapsto^{k+j}}\text{result}(M)
and
\text{result}(M)=\text{result}(N)
and
k+j<i+j
.

∎

{\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A,i}}}\subseteq\{\cdot\vdash V:A\}^{2}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt{\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B},i}}}\subseteq\{\cdot\,\,|\,\,\underline{B}\vdash S:\underline{F}(1+1)\}^{2}\\
\begin{array}[]{rcl}\cdot\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\cdot,i}}\cdot&=&\top\\
\gamma_{1},V_{1}/x\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\Gamma,x:A,i}}\gamma_{2},V_{2}/x&=&\gamma_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\Gamma,i}}\gamma_{2}\wedge V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A,i}}V_{2}\\
V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{0,i}}V_{2}&=&\bot\\
\texttt{inl}\,\,V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A+A^{\prime},i}}\texttt{inl}\,\,V_{2}&=&V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A,i}}V_{2}\\
\texttt{inr}\,\,V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A+A^{\prime},i}}\texttt{inr}\,\,V_{2}&=&V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A^{\prime},i}}V_{2}\\
()\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{1,i}}()&=&\top\\
(V_{1},V_{1}^{\prime})\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A\times A^{\prime},i}}(V_{2},V_{2}^{\prime})&=&V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A,i}}V_{2}\wedge V_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A^{\prime},i}}V_{2}^{\prime}\\
\texttt{roll}_{\mu X.A}\,\,V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\mu X.A,i}}\texttt{roll}_{\mu X.A}\,\,V_{2}&=&i=0\vee V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A[\mu X.A/X],i-1}}V_{2}\\
V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{U\underline{B},i}}V_{2}&=&\forall j\leq i,S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B},j}}S_{2}.~S_{1}[\texttt{force}\,\,V_{1}]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{j}}\text{result}(S_{2}[\texttt{force}\,\,V_{2}])\\
\\
S_{1}[\bullet V_{1}]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A\to\underline{B},i}}S_{1}[\bullet V_{2}]&=&V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A,i}}V_{2}\wedge S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B},i}}S_{2}\\
S_{1}[\pi_{1}\bullet]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B}\mathbin{\&}\underline{B}^{\prime},i}}S_{2}[\pi_{1}\bullet]&=&S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B},i}}S_{2}\\
S_{1}[\pi_{2}\bullet]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B}\mathbin{\&}\underline{B}^{\prime},i}}S_{2}[\pi_{2}\bullet]&=&S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B}^{\prime},i}}S_{2}\\
S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\top,i}}S_{2}&=&\bot\\
S_{1}[\texttt{unroll}\,\,\bullet]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\nu\underline{Y}.\underline{B},i}}S_{2}[\texttt{unroll}\,\,\bullet]&=&i=0\vee S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B}[\nu\underline{Y}.\underline{B}/\underline{Y}],i-1}}S_{2}\\
S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{F}A,i}}S_{2}&=&\forall j\leq i,V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A,j}}V_{2}.~S_{1}[\texttt{ret}\,\,V_{1}]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{j}}\text{result}(S_{2}[\texttt{ret}\,\,V_{2}])\end{array}

Figure 18.
Logical Relation from a Preorder
\trianglelefteq

Next, we define the (closed)
logical
preorder (for closed values/stacks) by induction on types and
the index
i
in figure
18
.
Specifically, for every
i
and value type
A
we define a relation

\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A,i}}
between closed values of type
A
because
these are the only ones that will be pattern-matched against at
runtime.
The relation is defined in a type-directed fashion, the intuition being
that we relate two positive values when they are built up in the same
way: i.e., they have the same introduction form and their subterms are
related.
For
\mu
, this definition would not be well-founded, so we decrement
the step index, giving up and relating the terms if
i=0
.
Finally
U
is the only negative value type, and so it is treated
differently.
A thunk
V:U\underline{B}
cannot be inspected by pattern matching, rather
the only way to interact with it is to force its evaluation.
By the definition of the operational semantics, this only ever occurs
in the step
S[\texttt{force}\,\,V]
, so (ignoring indices for a moment), we
should define
V_{1}\trianglelefteq V_{2}
to hold in this case when, given

S_{1}\trianglelefteq S_{2}
, the result of
S_{2}[\texttt{force}\,\,V_{2}]
is approximated
by
S_{1}[\texttt{force}\,\,V_{1}]
.
To incorporate the indices, we have to quantify over
j\leq i
in
this definition because we need to know that the values are related in
all futures, including ones where some other part of the term has been
reduced (consuming some steps).
Technically, this is crucial for making sure the relation is
downward-closed.
This is known as the
orthogonal
of the relation, and one
advantage of the CBPV language is that it makes the use of
orthogonality
explicit
in the type structure, analogous to the
benefits of using Nakano’s
later
modality
(
Nakano [n. d.]
)
for step indexing
(which we ironically do not do).

Next, we define when two
stacks
are related.
First, we define the relation only for two “closed” stacks, which
both have the same type of their hole
\underline{B}
and both have
“output” the observation type
\underline{F}2
.
The reason is that in evaluating a program
M
, steps always occur as

S[N]\mathrel{\Mapsto}S[N^{\prime}]
where
S
is a stack of this form.
An intuition is that for negative types, two stacks are related when
they start with the same elimination form and the remainder of the
stacks are related.
For
\nu
, we handle the step indices in the same way as for
\mu
.
For
\underline{F}A
, a stack
S[\bullet:\underline{F}A]
is strict in its input and
waits for its input to evaluate down to a value
\texttt{ret}\,\,V
, so two
stacks with
\underline{F}A
holes are related when in any future world, they
produce related behavior when given related values.

We note that in the CBV restriction of CBPV, the function type is
given by
U(A\to\underline{F}A^{\prime})
and the logical relation we have presented
reconstructs the usual definition that involves a double orthogonal.

Note that the definition is well-founded using the lexicographic
ordering on
(i,A)
and
(i,\underline{B})
: either the type reduces and the
index stays the same or the index reduces.
We extend the definition to contexts to
closing substitutions

pointwise: two closing substitutions for
\Gamma
are related at
i

if they are related at
i
for each
x:A\in\Gamma
.

The logical preorder for open terms is defined as usual by quantifying
over all related closing substitutions, but also over all stacks to the
observation type
\underline{F}(1+1)
:

Definition 6.20 (Logical Preorder).

For a divergence preorder
\trianglelefteq
, its step-indexed logical
preorder is

(1)

\Gamma\vDash M_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M_{2}\in\underline{B}
iff for every
\gamma_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\Gamma,i}}\gamma_{2}
and
S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B},i}}S_{2}
,
S_{1}[M_{1}[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M_{2}[\gamma_{2}]])
.

(2)

\Gamma\vDash V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}V_{2}\in A
iff
for every
\gamma_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\Gamma,i}}\gamma_{2}
,
V_{1}[\gamma_{1}]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A,i}}V_{2}[\gamma_{2}]

(3)

\Gamma\,\,|\,\,\underline{B}\vDash S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}S_{2}\in\underline{B}^{\prime}

iff for every
\gamma_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\Gamma,i}}\gamma_{2}
and

S_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B}^{\prime},i}}S_{2}^{\prime}
,
S_{1}^{\prime}[S_{1}[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B},i}}S_{2}^{\prime}[S_{2}[\gamma_{2}]])
.

We next want to prove that the logical preorder is a congruence
relation, i.e., the fundamental lemma of the logical relation.
This requires the easy lemma, that the relation on closed terms and
stacks is downward closed.

Lemma 6.21 (Logical Relation Downward Closure).

For any type
T
, if
j\leq i
then
\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{T,i}}\subseteq\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{T,j}}

Next, we show the fundamental theorem:

Theorem 6.22 (Logical Preorder is a Congruence).

For any divergence preorder, the logical preorder
E\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}E^{\prime}
is a congruence relation, i.e., it is closed under
applying any value/term/stack constructors to both sides.

Proof.

For each congruence rule

\frac{\Gamma\,\,|\,\,\Delta\vdash E_{1}\sqsubseteq E_{1}^{\prime}:T_{1}\cdots}{\Gamma^{\prime}\,\,|\,\,\Delta^{\prime}\vdash E_{c}\sqsubseteq E_{c}^{\prime}:T_{c}}

we prove for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
the validity of the rule

\frac{\Gamma\,\,|\,\,\Delta\vDash E_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}E_{1}^{\prime}\in T_{1}\cdots}{\Gamma\,\,|\,\,\Delta\vDash E_{c}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}E_{c}^{\prime}\in T_{c}}

(1)

\frac{}{\Gamma,x:A,\Gamma^{\prime}\vDash x\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}x\in A}
. Given
\gamma_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\Gamma,x:A,\Gamma^{\prime},i}}\gamma_{2}
,
then by definition
\gamma_{1}(x)\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A,i}}\gamma_{2}(x)
.

(2)

\frac{}{\Gamma\vDash\mho\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\in,\mho}}\underline{B}}
We
need to show
S_{1}[\mho]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[\mho])
. By
anti-reduction and strictness of stacks, it is sufficient to show

\mho\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\mho
. If
i=0
there is nothing to show,
otherwise, it follows by reflexivity of
\trianglelefteq
.

(3)

\frac{\Gamma\vDash V\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}V^{\prime}\in A\Gamma,x:A\vDash M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M^{\prime}\in\underline{B}}{\Gamma\vDash\texttt{let}\,\,x=V;M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\texttt{let}\,\,x=V^{\prime};{M^{\prime}}\in\underline{B}}

Each side takes a
0
-cost step, so by anti-reduction, this reduces to

S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1},V/x]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M^{\prime}[\gamma_{2},V^{\prime}/x]])

which follows by the assumption
\Gamma,x:A\vDash M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M^{\prime}\in\underline{B}

(4)

\frac{\Gamma\vDash V\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}V^{\prime}\in 0}{\Gamma\vDash\texttt{abort}\,\,V\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\texttt{abort}\,\,V^{\prime}\in\underline{B}}
.
By assumption, we get
V[\gamma_{1}]\mathrel{\lesssim^{i}_{0,\Bot}}V^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}]
, but this is a contradiction.

(5)

\frac{\Gamma\vDash V\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}V^{\prime}\in A_{1}}{\Gamma\vDash\texttt{inl}\,\,V\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\texttt{inl}\,\,V^{\prime}\in A_{1}+A_{2}}
.
Direct from assumption, rule for sums.

(6)

\frac{\Gamma\vDash V\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}V^{\prime}\in A_{2}}{\Gamma\vDash\texttt{inr}\,\,V\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\texttt{inr}\,\,V^{\prime}\in A_{1}+A_{2}}

Direct from assumption, rule for sums.

(7)

\frac{\Gamma\vDash V\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}V^{\prime}\in A_{1}+A_{2}\Gamma,x_{1}:A_{1}\vDash M_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M_{1}^{\prime}\in\underline{B}\Gamma,x_{2}:A_{2}\vDash M_{2}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M_{2}^{\prime}\in\underline{B}}{\Gamma\vDash\texttt{case}\,\,V\{x_{1}.M_{1}\,\,|\,\,x_{2}.M_{2}\}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\texttt{case}\,\,V^{\prime}\{x_{1}.M_{1}^{\prime}\,\,|\,\,x_{2}.M_{2}^{\prime}\}\in\underline{B}}

By case analysis of
V[\gamma_{1}]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}V^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}]
.

(a)

If
V[\gamma_{1}]=\texttt{inl}\,\,V_{1},V^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}]=\texttt{inl}\,\,V_{1}^{\prime}
with

V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A_{1},i}}V_{1}^{\prime}
, then taking
0
steps, by anti-reduction
the problem reduces to

S_{1}[M_{1}[\gamma_{1},V_{1}/x_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{1}[M_{1}[\gamma_{1},V_{1}/x_{1}]])

which follows by assumption.

(b)

For
inr
, the same argument.

(8)

\frac{}{\Gamma\vDash()\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}()\in 1}
Immediate by unit rule.

(9)

\frac{\Gamma\vDash V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}V_{1}^{\prime}\in A_{1}\Gamma\vDash V_{2}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}V_{2}^{\prime}\in A_{2}}{\Gamma\vDash(V_{1},V_{2})\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}(V_{1}^{\prime},V_{2}^{\prime})\in A_{1}\times A_{2}}

Immediate by pair rule.

(10)

\frac{\Gamma\vDash V\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}V^{\prime}\in A_{1}\times A_{2}\Gamma,x:A_{1},y:A_{2}\vDash M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M^{\prime}\in\underline{B}}{\Gamma\vDash\texttt{split}\,\,V\,\texttt{to}\,\,(x,y).M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\texttt{split}\,\,V^{\prime}\,\texttt{to}\,\,(x,y).M^{\prime}\in\underline{B}}

By
V\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A_{1}\times A_{2},i}}V^{\prime}
, we know
V[\gamma_{1}]=(V_{1},V_{2})
and
V^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}]=(V_{1}^{\prime},V_{2}^{\prime})
with
V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A_{1},i}}V_{1}^{\prime}
and
V_{2}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A_{2},i}}V_{2}^{\prime}
.
Then by anti-reduction, the problem reduces to

S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1},V_{1}/x,V_{2}/y]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{1}[M^{\prime}[\gamma_{1},V_{1}^{\prime}/x,V_{2}^{\prime}/y]])

which follows by assumption.

(11)

\frac{\Gamma\vDash V\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}V^{\prime}\in A[\mu X.A/X]}{\Gamma\vDash\texttt{roll}_{\mu X.A}\,\,V\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\texttt{roll}_{\mu X.A}\,\,V^{\prime}\in\mu X.A}

If
i=0
, we’re done. Otherwise
i=j+1
, and our assumption is
that
V[\gamma_{1}]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A[\mu X.A/X],j+1}}V^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}]
and we need to show
that
\texttt{roll}\,\,V[\gamma_{1}]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\mu X.A,j+1}}\texttt{roll}\,\,V^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}]
. By definition, we need to show
V[\gamma_{1}]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A[\mu X.A/X],j}}V^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}]
, which follows by downward-closure.

(12)

\frac{\Gamma\vDash V\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}V^{\prime}\in\mu X.A\Gamma,x:A[\mu X.A/X]\vDash M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M^{\prime}\in\underline{B}}{\Gamma\vDash\texttt{unroll}\,\,V\,\texttt{to}\,\,\texttt{roll}\,\,x.M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\texttt{unroll}\,\,V^{\prime}\,\texttt{to}\,\,\texttt{roll}\,\,x.M^{\prime}\in\underline{B}}

If
i=0
, then by triviality at
0
, we’re done.
Otherwise,
V[\gamma_{1}]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\mu X.A,j+1}}V^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}]
so

V[\gamma_{1}]=\texttt{roll}\,\,V_{\mu},V^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}]=\texttt{roll}\,\,V_{\mu}^{\prime}
with

V_{\mu}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A[\mu X.A/X],j}}V_{\mu}^{\prime}
. Then each side takes
1
step, so by anti-reduction it is sufficient to show

S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1},V_{\mu}/x]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{j}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M^{\prime}[\gamma_{2},V_{\mu}^{\prime}/x]])

which follows by assumption and downward closure of the stack, value relations.

(13)

\frac{\Gamma\vDash M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M^{\prime}\in\underline{B}}{\Gamma\vDash\texttt{thunk}\,\,M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\texttt{thunk}\,\,M^{\prime}\in U\underline{B}}
. We need to show

\texttt{thunk}\,\,M[\gamma_{1}]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{U\underline{B},i}}\texttt{thunk}\,\,M^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}]
, so let

S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B},j}}S_{2}
for some
j\leq i
, and we need to show

S_{1}[\texttt{force}\,\,\texttt{thunk}\,\,M_{1}[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{j}}\text{result}(S_{2}[\texttt{force}\,\,\texttt{thunk}\,\,M_{2}[\gamma_{2}]])

Then each side reduces in a
0
-cost step and it is sufficient to show

S_{1}[M_{1}[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{j}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M_{2}[\gamma_{2}]])

Which follows by downward-closure for terms and substitutions.

(14)

\frac{\Gamma\vDash V\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}V^{\prime}\in U\underline{B}}{\Gamma\vDash\texttt{force}\,\,V\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\texttt{force}\,\,V^{\prime}\in\underline{B}}
.

We need to show

S_{1}[\texttt{force}\,\,V[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[\texttt{force}\,\,V^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}]])
, which follows by the definition of
V[\gamma_{1}]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{U\underline{B},i}}V^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}]
.

(15)

\frac{\Gamma\vDash V\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}V^{\prime}\in A}{\Gamma\vDash\texttt{ret}\,\,V\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\texttt{ret}\,\,V^{\prime}\in\underline{F}A}

We need to show
S_{1}[\texttt{ret}\,\,V[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[\texttt{ret}\,\,V^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}]])
, which follows by the orthogonality definition of

S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{F}A,i}}S_{2}
.

(16)

\frac{\Gamma\vDash M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M^{\prime}\in\underline{F}A\Gamma,x:A\vDash N\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}N^{\prime}\in\underline{B}}{\Gamma\vDash\texttt{bind}\,\,x\leftarrow M;N\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\texttt{bind}\,\,x\leftarrow M^{\prime};{N^{\prime}}\in\underline{B}}
.

We need to show
\texttt{bind}\,\,x\leftarrow M[\gamma_{1}];{N[\gamma_{2}]}\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(\texttt{bind}\,\,x\leftarrow M^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}];{N^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}]})
.
By
M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M^{\prime}\in\underline{F}A
, it is sufficient to show that

\texttt{bind}\,\,x\leftarrow\bullet;{N[\gamma_{1}]}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{F}A,i}}\texttt{bind}\,\,x\leftarrow\bullet;{N^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}]}

So let
j\leq i
and
V\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A,j}}V^{\prime}
, then we need to show

\texttt{bind}\,\,x\leftarrow\texttt{ret}\,\,V;{N[\gamma_{1}]}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{F}A,j}}\texttt{bind}\,\,x\leftarrow\texttt{ret}\,\,V^{\prime};{N^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}]}

By anti-reduction, it is sufficient to show

N[\gamma_{1},V/x]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{j}}\text{result}(N^{\prime}[\gamma_{2},V^{\prime}/x])

which follows by anti-reduction for
\gamma_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\Gamma,i}}\gamma_{2}
and
N\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}N^{\prime}
.

(17)

\frac{\Gamma,x:A\vDash M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M^{\prime}\in\underline{B}}{\Gamma\vDash\lambda x:A.M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\lambda x:A.M^{\prime}\in A\to\underline{B}}

We need to show

S_{1}[\lambda x:A.M[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[\lambda x:A.M^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}]]).

By
S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A\to\underline{B},i}}S_{2}
, we know
S_{1}=S_{1}^{\prime}[\bullet V_{1}]
,
S_{2}=S_{2}^{\prime}[\bullet V_{2}]
with
S_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B},i}}S_{2}^{\prime}
and
V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A,i}}V_{2}
.
Then by anti-reduction it is sufficient to show

S_{1}^{\prime}[M[\gamma_{1},V_{1}/x]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}^{\prime}[M^{\prime}[\gamma_{2},V_{2}/x]])

which follows by
M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M^{\prime}
.

(18)

\frac{\Gamma\vDash M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M^{\prime}\in A\to\underline{B}\Gamma\vDash V\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}V^{\prime}\in A}{\Gamma\vDash M\,V\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M^{\prime}\,V^{\prime}\in\underline{B}}

We need to show

S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1}]\,V[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}]\,V^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}]])

so by
M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M^{\prime}
it is sufficient to show
S_{1}[\bullet V[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A\to\underline{B},i}}S_{2}[\bullet V^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}]]
which follows by definition and assumption that
V\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}V^{\prime}
.

(19)

\frac{}{\Gamma\vdash\{\}:\top}
We assume we are
given
S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\top,i}}S_{2}
, but this is a contradiction.

(20)

\frac{\Gamma\vDash M_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M_{1}^{\prime}\in\underline{B}_{1}\Gamma\vDash M_{2}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M_{2}^{\prime}\in\underline{B}_{2}}{\Gamma\vDash\{\pi\mapsto{M_{1}}\,\,|\,\,\pi^{\prime}\mapsto{M_{2}}\}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\{\pi\mapsto{M_{1}^{\prime}}\,\,|\,\,\pi^{\prime}\mapsto{M_{2}^{\prime}}\}\in\underline{B}_{1}\mathbin{\&}\underline{B}_{2}}

We need to show

S_{1}[\{\pi\mapsto{M_{1}[\gamma_{1}]}\,\,|\,\,\pi^{\prime}\mapsto{M_{2}[\gamma_{1}]}\}]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[\{\pi\mapsto{M_{1}^{\prime}[\gamma_{1}]}\,\,|\,\,\pi^{\prime}\mapsto{M_{2}^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}]}\}]).

We proceed by case analysis of
S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B}_{1}\mathbin{\&}\underline{B}_{2},i}}S_{2}

(a)

In the first possibility
S_{1}=S_{1}^{\prime}[\pi\bullet],S_{2}=S_{2}^{\prime}[\pi\bullet]
and
S_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B}_{1},i}}S_{2}^{\prime}
.
Then by anti-reduction, it is sufficient to show

S_{1}^{\prime}[M_{1}[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}^{\prime}[M_{1}^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}]])

which follows by
M_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M_{1}^{\prime}
.

(b)

Same as previous case.

(21)

\frac{\Gamma\vDash M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M^{\prime}\in\underline{B}_{1}\mathbin{\&}\underline{B}_{2}}{\Gamma\vDash\pi M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\pi M^{\prime}\in\underline{B}_{1}}

We need to show
S_{1}[\pi M[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[\pi M^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}]])
, which follows by
S_{1}[\pi\bullet]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B}_{1}\mathbin{\&}\underline{B}_{2},i}}S_{2}[\pi\bullet]
and
M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M^{\prime}
.

(22)

\frac{\Gamma\vDash M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M^{\prime}\in\underline{B}_{1}\mathbin{\&}\underline{B}_{2}}{\Gamma\vDash\pi^{\prime}M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\pi^{\prime}M^{\prime}\in\underline{B}_{2}}
Similar
to previous case.

(23)

\frac{\Gamma\vDash M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M^{\prime}\in\underline{B}[{\nu\underline{Y}.\underline{B}}/\underline{Y}]}{\Gamma\vDash\texttt{roll}_{\nu\underline{Y}.\underline{B}}\,\,M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\texttt{roll}_{\nu\underline{Y}.\underline{B}}\,\,M^{\prime}\in{\nu\underline{Y}.\underline{B}}}

We need to show that

S_{1}[\texttt{roll}_{\nu\underline{Y}.\underline{B}}\,\,M[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[\texttt{roll}_{\nu\underline{Y}.\underline{B}}\,\,M^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}]])

If
i=0
, we invoke triviality at
0
.
Otherwise,
i=j+1
and we know by
S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\nu\underline{Y}.\underline{B},j+1}}S_{2}
that

S_{1}=S_{1}^{\prime}[\texttt{unroll}\,\,\bullet]
and
S_{2}=S_{2}^{\prime}[\texttt{unroll}\,\,\bullet]
with
S_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B}[{\nu\underline{Y}.\underline{B}}/\underline{Y}],j}}S_{2}^{\prime}
, so by anti-reduction it is sufficient to show

S_{1}^{\prime}[M[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}^{\prime}[M^{\prime}[\gamma_{2}]])

which follows by
M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M^{\prime}
and downward-closure.

(24)

\frac{\Gamma\vDash M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M^{\prime}\in{\nu\underline{Y}.\underline{B}}}{\Gamma\vDash\texttt{unroll}\,\,M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\texttt{unroll}\,\,M^{\prime}\in\underline{B}[{\nu\underline{Y}.\underline{B}}/\underline{Y}]}

We need to show

S_{1}[\texttt{unroll}\,\,M]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[\texttt{unroll}\,\,M^{\prime}]),

which
follows because
S_{1}[\texttt{unroll}\,\,\bullet]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\nu\underline{Y}.\underline{B},i}}S_{2}[\texttt{unroll}\,\,\bullet]
and
M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M^{\prime}
.

∎

As a direct consequence we get the reflexivity of the relation

Corollary 6.23 (Reflexivity).

For any
\Gamma\vdash M:\underline{B}
, and
i\in\mathbb{N}
,

\Gamma\vDash M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M\in\underline{B}.

so we
have the following
strengthening
of the progress-and-preservation
type soundness theorem: because
\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}
only counts unrolling
steps, terms that never use
\mu
or
\nu
types (for example) are
guaranteed to terminate.

Corollary 6.24 (Unary LR).

For every program
\cdot\vdash M:\underline{F}2
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
,

M\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(M)

Proof.

By reflexivity,
\cdot\vDash M\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}M\in\underline{F}2
and by
definition
\bullet\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{F}2,i}}\bullet
, so
unrolling definitions we get
M\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(M)
.∎

Using reflexivity, we prove that the indexed relation between terms and
results recovers the original preorder in the limit as
i\to\omega
.
We write
\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{\omega}}
to mean the relation holds for every

i
, i.e.,
\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{\omega}}=\bigcap_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}
.

Corollary 6.25 (Limit Lemma).

For any divergence preorder
\trianglelefteq
,
\text{result}(M)\trianglelefteq R
iff
M\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{\omega}}R
.

Proof.

Two cases

(1)

If
\text{result}(M)\trianglelefteq R
then we need to show for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
,
M\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}R
. By the unary model lemma,

M\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(M)
, so the result follows by the
module lemma
6.15
.

(2)

If
M\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}R
for every
i
, then there are two
possibilities:
M
is always related to
R
because it takes
i

steps, or at some point
M
terminates.

(a)

If
M\mathrel{\Mapsto^{i}}M_{i}
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
, then

\text{result}(M)=\Omega
, so
\text{result}(M)\trianglelefteq R
because

\trianglelefteq
is a divergence preorder.

(b)

Otherwise there exists some
i\in\mathbb{M}
such that
M\mathrel{\Mapsto^{i}}\text{result}(M)
, so it follows by the module lemma

6.15
.

∎

Corollary 6.26 (Logical implies Contextual).

If
\Gamma\vDash E\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\omega}}E^{\prime}\in\underline{B}

then

\Gamma\vDash E\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{ctx}}}E^{\prime}\in\underline{B}
.

Proof.

Let
C
be a closing context. By congruence,
C[M]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\omega}}C[N]
, so using empty environment and stack,
C[M]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{\omega}}\text{result}(C[N])
and by the limit lemma, we have

\text{result}(C[M])\trianglelefteq\text{result}(C[N])
.
∎

In fact, we can prove the converse, that at least for the term case,
the logical preorder is
complete
with respect to the contextual
preorder, though we don’t use it.

Lemma 6.27 (Contextual implies Logical).

For any
\trianglelefteq
, if
\Gamma\vDash M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{ctx}}}N\in\underline{B}
, then
\Gamma\vDash M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\omega}}N\in\underline{B}
.

Proof.

Let
S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B},i}}S_{2}
and
\gamma_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\Gamma,i}}\gamma_{2}
. We need to show that

S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[N[\gamma_{2}]])

So we need to construct a
context
that when
M
or
N
is
plugged into the hole will reduce to the above.

To do this, first, we deconstruct the context

x_{1}:A_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:A_{n}=\Gamma
. Then we define
\cdot\vdash M^{\prime}:A_{1}\to\cdots\to A_{n}\to\underline{B}
as

\lambda x_{1}:A_{1}.\ldots\lambda x_{n}:A_{n}.M

And similarly define
N^{\prime}
. Then clearly

S[M^{\prime}\,V_{1}\,\cdots V_{n}]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{0}}S[M[V_{1}/x_{1},\ldots,V_{n}/x_{n}]]

so in particular

S[M^{\prime}\,\gamma(x_{1})\cdots\gamma(x_{n})]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{0}}S[M[\gamma]]

and similarly for
N^{\prime}
if
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
are all of the variables
in
\gamma
.

Then the proof proceeds by the following transitivity chain:

(
M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M
)

\displaystyle S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1}]]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2}]])

(reduction)

\displaystyle=\text{result}(S_{2}[M^{\prime}\,\gamma_{2}(x_{1})\,\cdots\,\gamma_{2}(x_{n})])

(
M\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{ctx}}}N
)

\displaystyle\trianglelefteq\text{result}(S_{2}[N^{\prime}\,\gamma_{2}(x_{1})\,\cdots\,\gamma_{2}(x_{n})])

(reduction)

\displaystyle=\text{result}(S_{2}[N[\gamma_{2}]])

So
S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[N[\gamma_{2}]])
by
the module lemma
6.15
.∎

This establishes that our logical relation can prove graduality, so it
only remains to show that our
inequational theory
implies our
logical relation.
Having already validated the congruence rules and reflexivity, we
validate the remaining rules of transitivity, error, substitution, and

\beta\eta
for each type constructor.
Other than the
\mho\sqsubseteq M
rule, all of these hold for any
divergence preorder.

For transitivity, with the unary model and limiting lemmas in hand, we
can prove that all of our logical relations (open and closed) are
transitive in the limit. To do this, we first prove the following kind
of “quantitative” transitivity lemma, and then transitivity in the
limit is a consequence.

Lemma 6.28 (Logical Relation is Quantitatively Transitive).

(1)

If
V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A,i}}V_{2}
and
V_{2}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A,\omega}}V_{3}
, then
V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A,i}}V_{3}

(2)

If
S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B},i}}S_{2}
and
S_{2}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B},\omega}}S_{3}
, then
S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B},i}}S_{3}

Proof.

Proof is by mutual lexicographic induction on the pair
(i,A)
or

(i,\underline{B})
. All cases are straightforward uses of the inductive
hypotheses except the shifts
U,\underline{F}
.

(1)

If
V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{U\underline{B},i}}V_{2}
and
V_{2}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{U\underline{B},\omega}}V_{3}
, then we need to show that
for any
S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B},j}}S_{2}
with
j\leq i
,

S_{1}[\texttt{force}\,\,V_{1}]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{j}}\text{result}(S_{2}[\texttt{force}\,\,V_{3}])

By reflexivity, we know
S_{2}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B},\omega}}S_{2}
, so by assumption

S_{2}[\texttt{force}\,\,V_{2}]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{\omega}}\text{result}(S_{2}[\texttt{force}\,\,V_{3}])

which by the limiting lemma
6.25
is equivalent to

\text{result}(S_{2}[\texttt{force}\,\,V_{2}])\trianglelefteq\text{result}(S_{2}[\texttt{force}\,\,V_{3}])

so then by the module lemma
6.15
, it is sufficient to show

S_{1}[\texttt{force}\,\,V_{1}]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{j}}\text{result}(S_{2}[\texttt{force}\,\,V_{2}])

which holds by assumption.

(2)

If
S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{F}A,i}}S_{2}
and
S_{2}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{F}A,\omega}}S_{3}
, then we need to show that for any
V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{j,A}}V_{2}
with
j\leq i
that

S_{1}[\texttt{ret}\,\,V_{1}]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{j}}\text{result}(S_{3}[\texttt{ret}\,\,V_{2}])

First by reflexivity, we know
V_{2}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A,\omega}}V_{2}
, so by assumption,

S_{2}[\texttt{ret}\,\,V_{2}]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{\omega}}\text{result}(S_{3}[\texttt{ret}\,\,V_{2}])

Which by the limit lemma
6.25
is equivalent to

\text{result}(S_{2}[\texttt{ret}\,\,V_{2}])\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{\omega}}\text{result}(S_{3}[\texttt{ret}\,\,V_{2}])

So by the module lemma
6.15
, it is sufficient to show

S_{1}[\texttt{ret}\,\,V_{1}]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{j}}\text{result}(S_{2}[\texttt{ret}\,\,V_{2}])

which holds by assumption.

∎

Lemma 6.29 (Logical Relation is Quantitatively Transitive (Open Terms)).

(1)

If
\gamma_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\Gamma,i}}\gamma_{2}
and
\gamma_{2}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\Gamma,\omega}}\gamma_{3}
, then
\gamma_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\Gamma,i}}\gamma_{3}

(2)

If
\Gamma\vDash M_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M_{2}\in\underline{B}
and

\Gamma\vDash M_{2}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\omega}}M_{3}\in\underline{B}
, then

\Gamma\vDash M_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}M_{3}\in\underline{B}
.

(3)

If
\Gamma\vDash V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}V_{2}\in A
and

\Gamma\vDash V_{2}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\omega}}V_{3}\in A
, then

\Gamma\vDash V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}V_{3}\in A
.

(4)

If
\Gamma\,\,|\,\,\bullet:\underline{B}\vDash S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}S_{2}\in\underline{B}^{\prime}
and

\Gamma\,\,|\,\,\bullet:\underline{B}\vDash S_{2}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\omega}}S_{3}\in\underline{B}^{\prime}
, then

\Gamma\,\,|\,\,\bullet:\underline{B}\vDash S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}S_{3}\in\underline{B}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

(1)

By induction on the length of the context, follows from closed value case.

(2)

Assume
\gamma_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\Gamma,i}}\gamma_{2}
and
S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B},i}}S_{2}
.
We need to show

S_{1}[M_{1}[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M_{3}[\gamma_{2}]])

by reflexivity and assumption, we know

S_{2}[M_{2}[\gamma_{2}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{\omega}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M_{3}[\gamma_{2}]])

and by limit lemma
6.25
, this is equivalent to

\text{result}(S_{2}[M_{2}[\gamma_{2}]])\trianglelefteq\text{result}(S_{2}[M_{3}[\gamma_{2}]])

so by the module lemma
6.15
it is sufficient to show

S_{1}[M_{1}[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M_{2}[\gamma_{2}]])

which follows by assumption.

(3)

Assume
\gamma_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\Gamma,i}}\gamma_{2}
. Then

V_{1}[\gamma_{1}]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A,i}}V_{2}[\gamma_{2}]
and by reflexivity

\gamma_{2}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\Gamma,\omega}}\gamma_{2}
so
V_{2}[\gamma_{2}]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A,\omega}}V_{3}[\gamma_{2}]
so the result holds by the closed case.

(4)

Stack case is essentially the same as the value case.

∎

Corollary 6.30 (Logical Relation is Transitive in the Limit).

(1)

If
\Gamma\vDash M_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\omega}}M_{2}\in\underline{B}
and

\Gamma\vDash M_{2}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\omega}}M_{3}\in\underline{B}
, then

\Gamma\vDash M_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\omega}}M_{3}\in\underline{B}
.

(2)

If
\Gamma\vDash V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\omega}}V_{2}\in A
and

\Gamma\vDash V_{2}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\omega}}V_{3}\in A
, then

\Gamma\vDash V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\omega}}V_{3}\in A
.

(3)

If
\Gamma\,\,|\,\,\bullet:\underline{B}\vDash S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\omega}}S_{2}\in\underline{B}^{\prime}
and

\Gamma\,\,|\,\,\bullet:\underline{B}\vDash S_{2}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\omega}}S_{3}\in\underline{B}^{\prime}
, then

\Gamma\,\,|\,\,\bullet:\underline{B}\vDash S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\omega}}S_{3}\in\underline{B}^{\prime}
.

Next, we verify the
\beta,\eta
equivalences hold as orderings each
way.

Lemma 6.31 (
\beta,\eta
).

For any divergence preorder, the
\beta,\eta

laws are valid for
\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\omega}}

Proof.

The
\beta
rules for all cases except recursive types are direct
from anti-reduction.

(1)

\mu X.A-\beta
:

(a)

We need to show

S_{1}[\texttt{unroll}\,\,\texttt{roll}_{\mu X.A}\,\,V[\gamma_{1}]\,\texttt{to}\,\,\texttt{roll}\,\,x.M[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2},V[\gamma_{2}]/x]])

The left side takes
1
step to
S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1},V[\gamma_{1}]/x]]
and we know

S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1},V[\gamma_{1}]/x]]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2},V[\gamma_{2}]/x]])

by assumption and reflexivity, so by anti-reduction we have

S_{1}[\texttt{unroll}\,\,\texttt{roll}_{\mu X.A}\,\,V[\gamma_{1}]\,\texttt{to}\,\,\texttt{roll}\,\,x.M[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i+1}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2},V[\gamma_{2}]/x]])

so the result follows by downward-closure.

(b)

For the other direction we need to show

S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1},V[\gamma_{1}]/x]]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[\texttt{unroll}\,\,\texttt{roll}_{\mu X.A}\,\,V[\gamma_{2}]\,\texttt{to}\,\,\texttt{roll}\,\,x.M[\gamma_{2}]])

Since results are invariant under steps, this is the same as

S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1},V[\gamma_{1}]/x]]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2},V[\gamma_{2}/x]]])

which follows by reflexivity and assumptions about the stacks
and substitutions.

(2)

\mu X.A-\eta
:

(a)

We need to show for any
\Gamma,x:\mu X.A\vdash M:\underline{B}
,
and appropriate substitutions and stacks,

S_{1}[\texttt{unroll}\,\,\texttt{roll}_{\mu X.A}\,\,{\gamma_{1}(x)}\,\texttt{to}\,\,\texttt{roll}\,\,y.M[\texttt{roll}_{\mu X.A}\,\,y/x][\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2}]])

By assumption,
\gamma_{1}(x)\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\mu X.A,i}}\gamma_{2}(x)
, so we know

\gamma_{1}(x)=\texttt{roll}_{\mu X.A}\,\,V_{1}

and

\gamma_{2}(x)=\texttt{roll}_{\mu X.A}\,\,V_{2}

so the left side takes a step:

\displaystyle S_{1}[\texttt{unroll}\,\,\texttt{roll}\,\,{\gamma_{1}(x)}\,\texttt{to}\,\,\texttt{roll}\,\,y.M[\texttt{roll}\,\,y/x][\gamma_{1}]]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\Mapsto^{1}}S_{1}[M[\texttt{roll}\,\,y/x][\gamma_{1}][V_{1}/y]]

\displaystyle=S_{1}[M[\texttt{roll}\,\,V_{1}/x][\gamma_{1}]]

\displaystyle=S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1}]]

and by reflexivity and assumptions we know

S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2}]])

so by anti-reduction we know

S_{1}[\texttt{unroll}\,\,\texttt{roll}_{\mu X.A}\,\,{\gamma_{1}(x)}\,\texttt{to}\,\,\texttt{roll}\,\,y.M[\texttt{roll}_{\mu X.A}\,\,y/x][\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i+1}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2}]])

so the result follows by downward closure.

(b)

Similarly, to show

S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[\texttt{unroll}\,\,\texttt{roll}_{\mu X.A}\,\,{\gamma_{2}(x)}\,\texttt{to}\,\,\texttt{roll}\,\,y.M[\texttt{roll}_{\mu X.A}\,\,y/x][\gamma_{2}]])

by the same reasoning as above,
\gamma_{2}(x)=\texttt{roll}_{\mu X.A}\,\,V_{2}
, so because result is invariant under reduction we need to show

S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2}]])

which follows by assumption and reflexivity.

(3)

\nu\underline{Y}.\underline{B}-\beta

(a)

We need to show

S_{1}[\texttt{unroll}\,\,\texttt{roll}_{\nu\underline{Y}.\underline{B}}\,\,M[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2}]])

By the operational semantics,

S_{1}[\texttt{unroll}\,\,\texttt{roll}_{\nu\underline{Y}.\underline{B}}\,\,M[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{1}}S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1}]]

and by reflexivity and assumptions

S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2}]]

so the result follows by anti-reduction and downward closure.

(b)

We need to show

S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[\texttt{unroll}\,\,\texttt{roll}_{\nu\underline{Y}.\underline{B}}\,\,M[\gamma_{2}]])

By the operational semantics and invariance of result under reduction this is equivalent to

S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2}]])

which follows by assumption.

(4)

\nu\underline{Y}.\underline{B}-\eta

(a)

We need to show

S_{1}[\texttt{roll}\,\,\texttt{unroll}\,\,M[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2}]])

by assumption,
S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\nu\underline{Y}.\underline{B},i}}S_{2}
, so

S_{1}=S_{1}^{\prime}[\texttt{unroll}\,\,\bullet]

and therefore the left side reduces:

\displaystyle S_{1}[\texttt{roll}\,\,\texttt{unroll}\,\,M[\gamma_{1}]]

\displaystyle=S_{1}^{\prime}[\texttt{unroll}\,\,\texttt{roll}\,\,\texttt{unroll}\,\,M[\gamma_{1}]]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\Mapsto^{1}}S_{1}^{\prime}[\texttt{unroll}\,\,M[\gamma_{1}]]

\displaystyle=S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1}]]

and by assumption and reflexivity,

S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2}]])

so the result holds by anti-reduction and downward-closure.

(b)

Similarly, we need to show

S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[\texttt{roll}\,\,\texttt{unroll}\,\,M[\gamma_{2}]])

as above,
S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\nu\underline{Y}.\underline{B},i}}S_{2}
, so we know

S_{2}=S_{2}^{\prime}[\texttt{unroll}\,\,\bullet]

so

\text{result}(S_{2}[\texttt{roll}\,\,\texttt{unroll}\,\,M[\gamma_{2}]])=\text{result}(S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2}]])

and the result follows by reflexivity, anti-reduction and downward closure.

(5)

0\eta
Let
\Gamma,x:0\vdash M:\underline{B}
.

(a)

We need to show

S_{1}[\texttt{absurd}\,\,\gamma_{1}(x)]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2}]])

By assumption
\gamma_{1}(x)\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{0,i}}\gamma_{2}(x)
but this is a contradiction

(b)

Other direction is the same contradiction.

(6)

+\eta
. Let
\Gamma,x:A_{1}+A_{2}\vdash M:\underline{B}

(a)

We need to show

S_{1}[\texttt{case}\,\,\gamma_{1}(x)\{x_{1}.M[\texttt{inl}\,\,x_{1}/x][\gamma_{1}]\,\,|\,\,x_{2}.M[\texttt{inr}\,\,x_{2}/x][\gamma_{1}]\}]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2}]])

by assumption

\gamma_{1}(x)\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A_{1}+A_{2},i}}\gamma_{2}(x)
, so either it’s
an
inl
or
inr
. The cases are symmetric so assume

\gamma_{1}(x)=\texttt{inl}\,\,V_{1}
.
Then

\displaystyle S_{1}[\texttt{case}\,\,\gamma_{1}(x)\{x_{1}.M[\texttt{inl}\,\,x_{1}/x][\gamma_{1}]\,\,|\,\,x_{2}.M[\texttt{inr}\,\,x_{2}/x][\gamma_{1}]\}]

\displaystyle=S_{1}[\texttt{case}\,\,(\texttt{inl}\,\,V_{1})\{x_{1}.M[\texttt{inl}\,\,x_{1}/x][\gamma_{1}]\,\,|\,\,x_{2}.M[\texttt{inr}\,\,x_{2}/x][\gamma_{1}]\}]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\Mapsto^{0}}S_{1}[M[\texttt{inl}\,\,V_{1}/x][\gamma_{1}]]

\displaystyle=S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1}]]

and so by anti-reduction it is sufficient to show

S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2}]]

which follows by reflexivity and assumptions.

(b)

Similarly, We need to show

\text{result}(S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1}]])\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[\texttt{case}\,\,\gamma_{2}(x)\{x_{1}.M[\texttt{inl}\,\,x_{1}/x][\gamma_{2}]\,\,|\,\,x_{2}.M[\texttt{inr}\,\,x_{2}/x][\gamma_{2}]\}])

and by assumption
\gamma_{1}(x)\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A_{1}+A_{2},i}}\gamma_{2}(x)
, so either it’s an
inl
or
inr
. The cases are
symmetric so assume
\gamma_{2}(x)=\texttt{inl}\,\,V_{2}
.
Then

S_{2}[\texttt{case}\,\,\gamma_{2}(x)\{x_{1}.M[\texttt{inl}\,\,x_{1}/x][\gamma_{2}]\,\,|\,\,x_{2}.M[\texttt{inr}\,\,x_{2}/x][\gamma_{2}]\}]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{0}}S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2}]]

So the result holds by invariance of result under reduction,
reflexivity and assumptions.

(7)

1\eta
Let
\Gamma,x:1\vdash M:\underline{B}

(a)

We need to show

S_{1}[M[()/x][\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2}]])

By assumption
\gamma_{1}(x)\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{1,i}}\gamma_{2}(x)
so
\gamma_{1}(x)=()
, so this is equivalent to

S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2}]])

which follows by reflexivity, assumption.

(b)

Opposite case is similar.

(8)

\times\eta
Let
\Gamma,x:A_{1}\times A_{2}\vdash M:\underline{B}

(a)

We need to show

S_{1}[\texttt{split}\,\,x\,\texttt{to}\,\,(x_{1},y_{1}).M[(x_{1},y_{1})/x][\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2}]])

By assumption
\gamma_{1}(x)\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A_{1}\times A_{2},i}}\gamma_{2}(x)
, so
\gamma_{1}(x)=(V_{1},V_{2})
, so

\displaystyle S_{1}[\texttt{split}\,\,x\,\texttt{to}\,\,(x_{1},y_{1}).M[(x_{1},y_{1})/x][\gamma_{1}]]

\displaystyle=S_{1}[\texttt{split}\,\,(V_{1},V_{2})\,\texttt{to}\,\,(x_{1},y_{1}).M[(x_{1},y_{1})/x][\gamma_{1}]]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\Mapsto^{0}}S_{1}[M[(V_{1},V_{2})/x][\gamma_{1}]]

\displaystyle=S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1}]]

So by anti-reduction it is sufficient to show

S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2}]])

which follows by reflexivity, assumption.

(b)

Opposite case is similar.

(9)

U\eta
Let
\Gamma\vdash V:U\underline{B}

(a)

We need to show that

\texttt{thunk}\,\,\texttt{force}\,\,V[\gamma_{1}]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{U\underline{B},i}}V[\gamma_{2}]

So assume
S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B},j}}S_{2}
for some
j\leq i
, then we need to show

S_{1}[\texttt{force}\,\,\texttt{thunk}\,\,\texttt{force}\,\,V[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{j}}\text{result}(S_{2}[\texttt{force}\,\,V[\gamma_{2}]])

The left side takes a step:

S_{1}[\texttt{force}\,\,\texttt{thunk}\,\,\texttt{force}\,\,V[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{0}}S_{1}[\texttt{force}\,\,V[\gamma_{1}]]

so by anti-reduction it is sufficient to show

S_{1}[\texttt{force}\,\,V[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{j}}\text{result}(S_{2}[\texttt{force}\,\,V[\gamma_{2}]])

which follows by assumption.

(b)

Opposite case is similar.

(10)

F\eta

(a)

We need to show that given
S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{F}A,i}}S_{2}
,

S_{1}[\texttt{bind}\,\,x\leftarrow\bullet;\texttt{ret}\,\,x]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{F}A,i}}S_{2}

So assume
V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A,j}}V_{2}
for some
j\leq i
, then we need to show

S_{1}[\texttt{bind}\,\,\texttt{ret}\,\,V_{1}\leftarrow\bullet;\texttt{ret}\,\,x]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{j}}\text{result}(S_{2}[\texttt{ret}\,\,V_{2}])

The left side takes a step:

S_{1}[\texttt{bind}\,\,\texttt{ret}\,\,V_{1}\leftarrow\bullet;\texttt{ret}\,\,x]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{0}}S_{1}[\texttt{ret}\,\,V_{1}]

so by anti-reduction it is sufficient to show

S_{1}[\texttt{ret}\,\,V_{1}]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{j}}\text{result}(S_{2}[\texttt{ret}\,\,V_{2}])

which follows by assumption

(b)

Opposite case is similar.

(11)

\to\eta
Let
\Gamma\vdash M:A\to\underline{B}

(a)

We need to show

S_{1}[(\lambda x:A.M[\gamma_{1}]\,x)]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2}]])

by assumption that
S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A\to\underline{B},i}}S_{2}
, we know

S_{1}=S_{1}^{\prime}[\bullet\,V_{1}]

so the left side takes a step:

\displaystyle S_{1}[(\lambda x:A.M[\gamma_{1}]\,x)]

\displaystyle=S_{1}^{\prime}[(\lambda x:A.M[\gamma_{1}]\,x)\,V_{1}]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\Mapsto^{0}}S_{1}^{\prime}[M[\gamma_{1}]\,V_{1}]

\displaystyle=S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1}]]

So by anti-reduction it is sufficient to show

S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2}]])

which follows by reflexivity, assumption.

(b)

Opposite case is similar.

(12)

\mathbin{\&}\eta
Let
\Gamma\vdash M:\underline{B}_{1}\mathbin{\&}\underline{B}_{2}

(a)

We need to show

S_{1}[\{\pi\mapsto{\pi M[\gamma_{1}]}\,\,|\,\,\pi^{\prime}\mapsto{\pi^{\prime}M[\gamma_{1}]}\}]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{1}[M[\gamma_{2}]])

by assumption,
S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B}_{1}\mathbin{\&}\underline{B}_{2},i}}S_{2}
so
either it starts with a
\pi
or
\pi^{\prime}
so assume that
S_{1}=S_{1}^{\prime}[\pi\bullet]
(
\pi^{\prime}
case is similar).
Then the left side reduces

\displaystyle S_{1}[\{\pi\mapsto{\pi M[\gamma_{1}]}\,\,|\,\,\pi^{\prime}\mapsto{\pi^{\prime}M[\gamma_{1}]}\}]

\displaystyle=S_{1}^{\prime}[\pi\{\pi\mapsto{\pi M[\gamma_{1}]}\,\,|\,\,\pi^{\prime}\mapsto{\pi^{\prime}M[\gamma_{1}]}\}]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\Mapsto^{0}}S_{1}^{\prime}[\pi M[\gamma_{1}]]

\displaystyle=S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1}]]

So by anti-reduction it is sufficient to show

S_{1}[M[\gamma_{1}]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M[\gamma_{2}]])

which follows by reflexivity, assumption.

(b)

Opposite case is similar.

(13)

\top\eta
Let
\Gamma\vdash M:\top

(a)

In either case, we assume we are given
S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\top,i}}S_{2}
, but this is a contradiction.

∎

Lemma 6.32 (Substitution Principles).

For any diverge-bottom preorder
\trianglelefteq
, the following are
valid

(1)

\frac{\Gamma\vDash V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}V_{2}\in A\Gamma,x:A\vDash V_{1}^{\prime}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{V}}_{2}^{\prime}\in A^{\prime}}{\Gamma\vDash V_{1}^{\prime}[V_{1}/x]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{V}}_{2}^{\prime}[V_{2}/x]\in A^{\prime}}

(2)

\frac{\Gamma\vDash V_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{i}}V_{2}\in A\Gamma,x:A\vDash M_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{M}}_{2}\in\underline{B}}{\Gamma\vDash M_{1}[V_{1}/x]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{M}}_{2}[V_{2}/x]\in\underline{B}}

Proof.

We do the term case, the value case is similar. Given
\gamma_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\Gamma,i}}\gamma_{2}
, we have
V_{1}[\gamma_{1}]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{A,i}}V_{2}[\gamma_{2}]
so

\gamma_{1},V_{1}[\gamma_{1}]/x\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\Gamma,x:A,i}}\gamma_{2},V_{2}[\gamma_{2}]/x

and by associativity of substitution

M_{1}[V_{1}/x][\gamma_{1}]=M_{1}[\gamma_{1},V_{1}[\gamma_{1}]/x]

and similarly for
M_{2}
, so if
S_{1}\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\underline{B},i}}S_{2}
then

S_{1}[M_{1}[\gamma_{1},V_{1}[\gamma_{1}]/x]]\mathrel{\trianglelefteq^{i}}\text{result}(S_{2}[M_{2}[\gamma_{2},V_{2}[\gamma_{2}]/x]])

∎

For errors, the strictness axioms hold for any
\trianglelefteq
, but the axiom that

\mho
is a least element is specific to the definitions of

\mathrel{\preceq\sqsubseteq},\sqsubseteq\succeq

Lemma 6.33 (Error Rules).

For any divergence preorder
\trianglelefteq
and appropriately
typed
S,M
,

S[\mho]\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\omega}}\mho\mho\mathrel{{\trianglelefteq}^{\text{log}}_{\omega}}S[\mho]\mho\mathrel{{\mathrel{\preceq\sqsubseteq}}^{\text{log}}_{\omega}}MM\mathrel{{\preceq\sqsupseteq}^{\text{log}}_{\omega}}\mho

Proof.

(1)

It is sufficient by the limit lemma to show
\text{result}(S[\mho])\trianglelefteq\mho
which holds by reflexivity because
S[\mho]\mathrel{\Mapsto^{0}}\mho
.

(2)

We need to show
S[\mho]\mathrel{\mathrel{\preceq\sqsubseteq}^{i}}R
for arbitrary
R
,
so by the limit lemma it is sufficient to show
\mho\mathrel{\preceq\sqsubseteq}R
, which is true by definition.

(3)

By the limit lemma it is sufficient to show
R\mathrel{\preceq\sqsupseteq}\mho
which is true by definition.

∎

The lemmas we have proved cover all of the inequality rules of CBPV, so
applying them with
\trianglelefteq
chosen to be
\preceq\sqsubseteq
and

\preceq\sqsupseteq
gives

Lemma 6.34 (
\mathrel{\preceq\sqsubseteq}
and
\mathrel{\sqsubseteq\succeq}
are Models of CBPV).

If
\Gamma\,\,|\,\,\Delta\vdash E\sqsubseteq E^{\prime}:\underline{B}
then

\Gamma\,\,|\,\,\Delta\vDash E\mathrel{\mathrel{\preceq\sqsubseteq}^{\omega}}E^{\prime}\in\underline{B}
and

\Gamma\,\,|\,\,\Delta\vDash E^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\preceq\sqsupseteq}^{\omega}}E\in\underline{B}
.

Because logical implies contextual equivalence, we can
conclude with the main theorem:

Theorem 6.35 (Contextual Approximation/Equivalence Model CBPV).

If
\Gamma\,\,|\,\,\Delta\vdash E\sqsubseteq E^{\prime}:T
then

\Gamma\,\,|\,\,\Delta\vDash E\mathrel{{\sqsubseteq}^{\text{ctx}}}E^{\prime}\in T
;
if

{\Gamma\,\,|\,\,\Delta\vdash E\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}E^{\prime}:T}
then

{\Gamma\,\,|\,\,\Delta\vDash E\mathrel{{=}^{\text{ctx}}}E^{\prime}\in T}
.

Proof.

For the first part, from Lemma
6.34
,
we have
E\mathrel{\mathrel{\preceq\sqsubseteq}^{\omega}}E^{\prime}
and
E^{\prime}\mathrel{\mathrel{\preceq\sqsupseteq}^{\omega}}E
. By Lemma
6.26
, we then have

E\mathrel{{\preceq\sqsubseteq}^{\text{ctx}}}E^{\prime}
and
E^{\prime}\mathrel{{\preceq\sqsupseteq}^{\text{ctx}}}E
. Finally, by Corollary
6.13
,
E\mathrel{{\sqsubseteq}^{\text{ctx}}}E^{\prime}\text{ iff }E\mathrel{{\preceq\sqsubseteq}^{\text{ctx}}}E^{\prime}\text{and
}E(\mathrel{{(\preceq\sqsupseteq)}^{\text{ctx}}})^{\circ}E^{\prime}
, so we have the result.

For the second part, applying the first part twice gives
E\mathrel{{\sqsubseteq}^{\text{ctx}}}E^{\prime}
and
E^{\prime}\mathrel{{\sqsubseteq}^{\text{ctx}}}E
, and we concluded in
Corollary
6.13
that this coincides with
contextual equivalence.∎

7.
Discussion and Related Work

In this paper, we have given a logic for reasoning about gradual
programs in a mixed call-by-value/call-by-name language, shown that
the axioms uniquely determine almost all of the contract translation
implementing runtime casts, and shown that the axiomatics is sound for
contextual equivalence/approximation in an operational model.

In immediate future work, we believe it is straightforward to add
inductive/coinductive types and obtain similar unique cast
implementation theorems
(e.g.
\langle{\mathtt{list}(A^{\prime})}\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{-30.0}{$\leftarrowtail$}}{\mathtt{list}(A)}\rangle\mathrel{\sqsupseteq\sqsubseteq}\mathtt{map}\langle{A^{\prime}}\mathrel{\rotatebox[origin]{-30.0}{$\leftarrowtail$}}{A}\rangle
). Additionally, since more efficient cast
implementations such as optimized cast calculi (the lazy variant in

Herman
et al. 2010
) and threesome
casts
(
Siek and Wadler 2010
)
, are equivalent to the lazy contract
semantics, they should also be models of GTT, and if so we could use GTT
to reason about program transformations and optimizations in them.

Applicability of Cast Uniqueness Principles

The cast uniqueness principles given in theorem

3.15
are theorems in the formal logic of Gradual
Type Theory, and so there is a question of to what languages the
theorem applies.
The theorem applies to any
model
of gradual type theory, such
as the models we have constructed using call-by-push-value given in
Sections
4
,
5
,
6
.
We conjecture that simple call-by-value and call-by-name gradual
languages are also models of GTT, by extending the translation of
call-by-push-value into call-by-value and call-by-name in the appendix
of Levy’s monograph
(
Levy 2003
)
.
In order for the theorem to apply, the language must validate an
appropriate version of the
\eta
principles for the types.
So for example, a call-by-value language that has reference equality
of functions does
not
validate even the value-restricted
\eta

law for functions, and so the case for functions does not apply.
It is a well-known issue that in the presence of pointer equality of
functions, the lazy semantics of function casts is not compatible with
the graduality property, and our uniqueness theorem provides a
different perspective on this phenomenon

(
Findler
et al. 2004
;
Strickland et al. 2012
;
Siek
et al. 2015a
)
.
However, we note that the cases of the uniqueness theorem for each
type connective are completely
modular
: they rely only on the
specification of casts and the
\beta,\eta
principles for the
particular connective, and not on the presence of any other types,
even the dynamic types.
So even if a call-by-value language may have reference equality
functions, if it has the
\eta
principle for strict pairs, then the
pair cast must be that of Theorem
3.15
.

Next, we consider the applicability to non-eager languages.
Analogous to call-by-value, our uniqueness principle should apply to
simple
call-by-name
gradual languages, where full
\eta

equality for functions is satisfied, but
\eta
equality for booleans
and strict pairs requires a “stack restriction” dual to the value
restriction for call-by-value function
\eta
.
We are not aware of any call-by-name gradual languages, but there is
considerable work on
contracts
for non-eager languages,
especially Haskell
(
Hinze
et al. 2006
;
Xu
et al. 2009
)
.
However, we note that Haskell is
not
a call-by-name language in
our sense for two reasons.
First, Haskell uses call-by-need evaluation where results of
computations are memoized. However, when only considering Haskell’s
effects (error and divergence), this difference is not observable so
this is not the main obstacle.
The bigger difference between Haskell and call-by-name is that Haskell
supports a
seq
operation that enables the programmer to force
evaluation of a term to a value.
This means Haskell violates the function
\eta
principle because

\Omega
will cause divergence under
seq
, whereas
\lambda x.\Omega
will not.
This is a crucial feature of Haskell and is a major source of
differences between implementations of lazy contracts, as noted in

Degen
et al. 2012
.
We can understand this difference by using a different translation
into call-by-push-value: what Levy calls the “lazy paradigm”, as
opposed to call-by-name
(
Levy 2003
)
.
Simply put, connectives are interpreted as in call-by-value, but with
the addition of extra thunks
UF
, so for instance the lazy function
type
A\to B
is interpreted as
UFU(UFA\to FB)
and the extra
UFU

here is what causes the failure of the call-by-name
\eta
principle.
With this embedding and the uniqueness theorem, GTT produces a
definition for lazy casts, and the definition matches the work of

Xu
et al. 2009
when restricting to non-dependent contracts.

Comparing Soundness Principles for Cast Semantics

Greenman and
Felleisen 2018
gives a spectrum of
differing syntactic type soundness theorems for different semantics of
gradual typing.
